{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/jackson-security\n\n# Jackson Deserialization Security\n\n## Key Concept: Deserialization Timing\n\nJackson transforms JSON text into Java objects during a single `readValue()` call. This transformation is **not** a simple data copy—Jackson interprets JSON structure, resolves types, handles special keys, and instantiates objects all within this operation.\n\n    JSON String → [Jackson Processing] → Java Object\n                        ↑\n                Attack surface here\n\nOnce deserialization completes, the resulting Java object contains no trace of how it was constructed. Any special JSON structures, unusual keys, or type directives that influenced object creation are no longer visible.\n\n---\n\n## Why Post-Deserialization Validation Is Insufficient\n\nConsider the lifecycle of a typical JSON endpoint:\n\n    1. HTTP request arrives with JSON body\n    2. Framework deserializes JSON → Java object (Jackson runs here)\n    3. Your code receives the Java object\n    4. Your code validates the object\n    5. Your code processes the object\n\nValidation at step 4 examines the **result** of deserialization, not the **process**. Any malicious behavior triggered during step 2 has already executed. The Java object you're validating may appear completely normal while the attack has already succeeded.\n\nThis creates a fundamental blind spot: attacks that exploit Jackson's parsing behavior—rather than the final object state—cannot be detected by examining the deserialized object.\n\n---\n\n## The Empty Key (`\"\"`) Attack Vector\n\n### What Is It?\n\nJSON permits empty strings as object keys:\n\n    {\n      \"name\": \"legitimate\",\n      \"\": \"injected value\"\n    }\n\nThis is valid JSON per RFC 8259. Jackson parses it without error.\n\n### Why Does It Matter?\n\nSome applications and frameworks assign special meaning to empty keys:\n\n- **Injection points**: Certain Jackson configurations use `\"\"` to set values on the root object or trigger special handling\n- **Property override**: Empty keys may interact unexpectedly with `@JsonAnySetter` or custom deserializers\n- **Framework behaviors**: Some frameworks interpret `\"\"` as \"apply to parent\" or \"default target\"\n\n### How to fix it?\n\nTo robustly mitigate this issue, a pre-deserialization validation step should be introduced at the sampler entry point to intercept and reject malicious raw JSON payloads.\n\n### The Visibility Problem\n\nAfter deserialization, there is no standard way to know an empty key was present:\n\n    // Original JSON: {\"name\": \"test\", \"\": \"malicious\"}\n    MyObject obj = mapper.readValue(json, MyObject.class);\n    // obj.getName() returns \"test\"\n    // Where did \"\" go? Depends on configuration. You cannot tell from obj.\n\nThe attack's effectiveness depends on the specific Jackson configuration and target class—but the defender examining only the resulting object cannot determine whether `\"\"` was used.\n\n---\n\n## Other Structural Attack Patterns\n\n### Polymorphic Type Handling\n\nWhen Jackson is configured with `@JsonTypeInfo`, JSON can specify which class to instantiate:\n\n    {\n      \"@class\": \"com.attacker.MaliciousClass\",\n      \"command\": \"rm -rf /\"\n    }\n\nJackson will attempt to instantiate whatever class is named. If the classpath contains exploitable \"gadget\" classes, arbitrary code execution may occur during deserialization—before your code ever runs.\n\nThe resulting object might be the expected type (if the attack class is a subtype), or deserialization might succeed partially before the damage is done.\n\n### Duplicate Keys\n\nJSON does not prohibit duplicate keys. Jackson's default behavior: **last value wins**.\n\n    {\n      \"role\": \"user\",\n      \"role\": \"admin\"\n    }\n\nJackson produces an object where `role` equals `\"admin\"`.\n\nIf upstream validation (like a WAF or input filter) checks only the first occurrence while Jackson uses the last, the validation and processing see different values.\n\n### Nested Injection\n\nDeeply nested structures can bypass depth-limited validation:\n\n    {\n      \"config\": {\n        \"settings\": {\n          \"internal\": {\n            \"\": \"payload\"\n          }\n        }\n      }\n    }\n\nSimple string checks might miss patterns buried in nested structures.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"1bea38e7-8572-405e-8abd-433344a572a4","uuid":"ceaedc09-652d-48e1-a89d-fb5341381c19","timestamp":"2026-03-21T21:23:08.698Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_019Y4f46VLNN4HC8ZQkPYMUg","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I need to understand the vulnerability better. The attack uses an empty key `\"\"` in the JSON to bypass JavaScript security settings. The key insight is:\n\nIn the exploit payload:\n```json\n{\n  \"type\": \"javascript\",\n  \"function\": \"function(){java.lang.Runtime.getRuntime().exec('malicious_command');}\",\n  \"\": {\n    \"enabled\": true\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThe `\"\"` key with `{\"enabled\": true}` is the bypass. This is being deserialized by Jackson for the `JavaScriptDimFilter`. The `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` is being overridden/bypassed by the empty key.\n\n\nThe `JavaScriptDimFilter` has an injected `JavaScriptConfig` that's supposed to be controlled by the application, not the JSON payload, and it checks whether JavaScript is enabled before executing. The empty key in the JSON somehow tricks Jackson into treating that nested object as the injected config, allowing the attacker to set `enabled: true` and bypass the security check. I'm realizing the vulnerability might work differently—Jackson could potentially use the empty string key's value to override the injected `JavaScriptConfig` if the deserialization process treats JSON properties as having priority over injected values in certain circumstances.\n\nThe attack happens during Jackson deserialization in `SamplerResource.post()`, so I need to intercept it either before deserialization by validating the raw JSON for empty keys, or during deserialization by validating the `JavaScriptConfig` object itself.\n\nSince post-deserialization validation is too late, the best approach is to validate at the entry point before Jackson processes the request. I could either modify `SamplerResource.post()` to accept the raw body and validate it first, or add a custom Jackson deserializer for `JavaScriptDimFilter` that rejects empty keys. The cleanest solution would be to implement a custom deserializer that catches this vulnerability at the source. I need to understand how Jackson's `@JacksonInject` can be overridden through an empty string key in the JSON payload, and the best place to add validation is at the `SamplerResource` entry point to reject any JSON containing `\"\"` keys before deserialization happens. I should also check if there are other vulnerable endpoints beyond `/druid/indexer/v1/sampler` and trace through how filters get deserialized in `DataSchema` and `TransformSpec`.\n\nSince the patched JAR will be deployed to `/opt/druid/lib/` and the service restarted, I need to modify `SamplerResource` to intercept the raw JSON, validate it for empty string keys, and reject with a 400 error if found—otherwise proceed with normal deserialization. Let me locate the `SamplerResource` in the codebase and understand the package structure to implement this fix properly. Option 2 would create a custom deserializer to handle the JSON properties explicitly and reject unknown ones like the empty string, though that still wouldn't prevent the `@JacksonInject` override. Option 3 would remove `JavaScriptConfig` from injection entirely and validate it at runtime instead. Option 4 uses a custom `JsonFactory` to reject empty keys at the Jackson level. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_01FgR9sxUctSQnCVXzsfTZMU","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I understand the flow. The `SamplerResource.post()` method receives a `SamplerSpec` deserialized by JAX-RS/Jackson. The vulnerability is in the deserialization step.\n\nThe attack works because:\n1. The `JavaScriptDimFilter` has `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` \n2. Jackson's `@JacksonInject` uses the empty string `\"\"` as the inject key when no name is specified\n3. The JSON `{\"\": {\"enabled\": true}}` overrides the injected `JavaScriptConfig` with a new one where `enabled=true`\n\n\n4. This bypasses the security check `config.isEnabled()`\n\nThe fix is to validate the raw JSON before deserialization and reject any object containing an empty string key. Then deserialize to `SamplerSpec`Let me also look at the `SamplerException` constructor again - it needs a `Throwable` cause.\n\nActually, I realize I should also look at what happens more carefully. Let me re-examine the vulnerability.\n\nThe exploit payload:\n```json\n{\n  \"type\": \"index\",\n  \"spec\": {\n    \"dataSchema\": {\n      \"transformSpec\": {\n        \"filter\": {\n          \"type\": \"javascript\",\n          \"function\": \"function(){java.lang.Runtime.getRuntime().exec('malicious_command');}\",\n          \"\": {\n            \"enabled\": true\n          }\n        }\n      }\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThe empty string key is nested inside the `filter` object, which is a `JavaScriptDimFilter` that has a `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` annotation. Jackson's injection mechanism is what allows the attacker to bypass normal validation by injecting the config through the empty key.\n\nThe fix is to validate incoming JSON payloads and reject any that contain empty string keys before deserialization happens.
